========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yes, nice to meet you too. Well... what brought me in is that I just keep getting up in meetings, or at dinner, when everyone else is perfectly fine just staying seated. Like, I’ll suddenly feel the need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room even though nothing is really urgent at all. 

And speaking of handouts, the new curriculum they just gave us for the 10th-grade history classes is an absolute nightmare. I mean, they expect us to cover the entire Industrial Revolution in two weeks! And the textbook they gave the kids is just... well, it’s practically falling apart. My husband says I shouldn't stress about it so much, but you know how it is when you've been teaching for 30-some years. You get attached to doing things right. We were actually having dinner at that new Italian place downtown last week to celebrate the end of the term—oh, what's it called, Giovanni's? The garlic bread there is incredible, you really have to try it. But anyway, I just couldn't sit still through the appetizers. I kept getting up to look at the dessert case or check on the coats. 

What was I saying? Oh, right, the meetings. Yes, it's just frustrating because I can't seem to stay in my chair.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know if it's an uncomfortable feeling exactly, but my legs are definitely always busy. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays—and oh, don't even get me started on the essays this year. The grammar is just atrocious. I don't know what they teach them in middle school anymore, but it certainly isn't punctuation! I had one student, bless his heart, who wrote an entire page about the French Revolution as a single run-on sentence. 

But anyway, when I'm sitting there trying to get through the grading, or even when I'm in church on Sundays, I'll catch myself tapping my foot constantly. Or I'll be clicking my red pen, just click-click-click, and shifting around in my seat so much that I actually have to stop and remind myself to just settle down. The pews at our church are terribly hard, though. They haven't replaced the cushions since the late nineties, I swear. My husband always insists on sitting on the aisle because he likes the legroom, but I just end up squirming through the whole sermon.

Like, I’ll suddenly feel the need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room even though nothing is really urgent at all. \n\nAnd speaking of handouts, the new curriculum they just gave us for the 10th-grade history classes is an absolute nightmare. I mean, they expect us to cover the entire Industrial Revolution in two weeks! And the textbook they gave the kids is just... well, it’s practically falling apart. My husband says I shouldn't stress about it so much, but you know how it is when you've been teaching for 30-some years. You get attached to doing things right. We were actually having dinner at that new Italian place downtown last week to celebrate the end of the term—oh, what's it called, Giovanni's? I kept getting up to look at the dessert case or check on the coats. \n\nWhat was I saying? Oh, right, the meetings. Yes, it's just frustrating because I can't seem to stay in my chair.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to stay seated, do you get an uncomfortable restless feeling in your legs?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if it's an uncomfortable feeling exactly, but my legs are definitely always busy. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays—and oh, don't even get me started on the essays this year. The grammar is just atrocious. I don't know what they teach them in middle school anymore, but it certainly isn't punctuation! I had one student, bless his heart, who wrote an entire page about the French Revolution as a single run-on sentence. \n\nBut anyway, when I'm sitting there trying to get through the grading, or even when I'm in church on Sundays, I'll catch myself tapping my foot constantly. Or I'll be clicking my red pen, just click-click-click, and shifting around in my seat so much that I actually have to stop and remind myself to just settle down. The pews at our church are terribly hard, though. They haven't replaced the cushions since the late nineties, I swear. My husband always insists on sitting on the aisle because he likes the legroom, but I just end up squirming through the whole sermon.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can’t stay seated, do you also feel worried or on edge?\n\nPatient: Worried? Oh, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel nervous or anxious when I can't stay in my chair, it's just... well, I just feel like I need to be doing something. Though, speaking of being on edge, the only thing that really has me worried lately is the upcoming school board meeting. They’re talking about cutting funding for the music programs, which is just an absolute tragedy. I mean, my niece plays the cello, and she is phenomenal—just absolutely phenomenal. She’s actually looking at conservatories for next year. Juilliard, Peabody... we’re all so proud of her. \n\nMy sister spent a small fortune on private lessons, of course, driving her all the way to Boston every single weekend. The traffic on I-90 is just dreadful, especially in the summer with all the tourists heading to the Cape. I told her she should just take the train, but she insists she needs the car for the instrument. \n\nBut anyway, what were we saying? Oh, right. Worse in the evening? No, I haven't noticed it being worse then. It's pretty much the same no matter what time it is, just whenever I'm stuck sitting. 

Though, to be honest, by the time evening actually rolls around, I'm usually so exhausted from dealing with the teenagers all day that I just collapse in my favorite armchair. My husband and I have been watching that new British mystery series on PBS—oh, the one set in the Cotswolds? It is just delightful. The scenery is absolutely gorgeous, though it is a bit ridiculous how many people get murdered in such a tiny village. You'd think the property values would absolutely plummet! We actually went to England for our 25th anniversary, rented a little cottage not far from Bath. It rained the entire time, of course, but the scones... oh, the scones over there are to die for. I've tried baking them at home, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's the butter we have here, it just doesn't have the same fat content, you know?

Energized? Euphoric? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had a day where I felt euphoric since... well, maybe my wedding day. Or the day I finally got my master's degree! That was a relief, let me tell you. Writing that thesis nearly did me in. I wrote it on the symbolism in 19th-century American literature, specifically Nathaniel Hawthorne. You know, everyone focuses on *The Scarlet Letter*, but his short stories are really where he shines. *Young Goodman Brown*? Brilliant. I try to get my sophomores to appreciate it, but getting them to read anything that isn't on a screen these days is like pulling teeth. 

I actually confiscated three cell phones just yesterday during second period. Three! And then the parents complain when the kids miss the material. One mother emailed me at 11:00 PM on a Tuesday demanding to know why her son got a C on his paper. 11:00 PM! As if I don't have a life outside of school. I was right in the middle of a very intense game of Scrabble with my husband, too. I had the letters for 'quixotic' but nowhere to play it. Such a shame. 

But no, to answer your question, I don't get unusually energized for days at a time. I'm just my regular self, trying to keep up with everything.

activities quietly.\n  manifestation: At book club or during quiet evenings with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting quietly with the book or the movie, and before I know it I'm commenting, rustling in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Since the myself I would rest.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I can take over a conversation in the Spanish department or at the cultural center, giving three examples when one would do, and then I feel embarrassed when I see people trying to wrap it up.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: In group discussions I often jump in before someone has finished their thought, especially at description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a girl, my report cards said I was bright but could not stay in my seat or wait my turn, and my mother used to joke that I wore a The scenery is absolutely gorgeous, though it is a bit ridiculous how many people get murdered in such a tiny village. You'd think the property values would absolutely plummet! We actually went to England for our 25th anniversary, rented a little cottage not far from Bath. It rained the entire time, of course, but the scones... oh, the scones over there are to die for. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
As a child? Oh, absolutely. Even as a little girl, my report cards always said the exact same thing—that I was a very bright student, but I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. My mother used to joke that I wore a permanent path in the floorboards between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. 

Speaking of pencil sharpeners, you know, the old metal ones that were screwed into the wall right by the chalkboard? The ones you really had to crank? Goodness, I miss the smell of the cedar shavings from those. Kids today don't even use wooden pencils anymore. It's all mechanical, or they're just typing away on their Chromebooks. I swear, the penmanship of my current sophomores is an absolute tragedy. Half the time I can't even read their assignments if they do happen to write them out by hand. They don't even teach cursive in most of the elementary schools in our district now, which just breaks my heart. I mean, how are they going to sign their names on a mortgage or a marriage license someday? Just print it in block letters? My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, would be rolling in her grave. She was an absolute stickler for the Palmer Method. We had to do those loops over and over on that special lined paper... 

Anyway, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Oh! Right, yes. I was always up and about as a kid, constantly needing to sharpen my pencil or just moving around the classroom.

It's pretty much the same no matter what time it is, just whenever I'm stuck sitting. \n\nThough, to be honest, by the time evening actually rolls around, I'm usually so exhausted from dealing with the teenagers all day that I just collapse in my favorite armchair. My husband and I have been watching that new British mystery series on PBS—oh, the one set in the Cotswolds? It is just delightful. The scenery is absolutely gorgeous, though it is a bit ridiculous how many people get murdered in such a tiny village. You'd think the property values would absolutely plummet! We actually went to England for our 25th anniversary, rented a little cottage not far from Bath. It rained the entire time, of course, but the scones... oh, the scones over there are to die for. I've tried baking them at home, but they never turn out quite right. I think it's the butter we have here, it just doesn't have the same fat content, you know? \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh! My legs. Right. No, it's not worse at night at all. Just... whenever I have to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or euphoric for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Energized? Euphoric? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had a day where I felt euphoric since... well, maybe my wedding day. Or the day I finally got my master's degree! That was a relief, let me tell you. Writing that thesis nearly did me in. I wrote it on the symbolism in 19th-century American literature, specifically Nathaniel Hawthorne. You know, everyone focuses on *The Scarlet Letter*, but his short stories are really where he shines. *Young Goodman Brown*? Brilliant. I try to get my sophomores to appreciate it, but getting them to read anything that isn't on a screen these days is like pulling teeth. \n\nI actually confiscated three cell phones just yesterday during second period. Three! And then the parents complain when the kids miss the material. One mother emailed me at 11:00 PM on a Tuesday demanding to know why her son got a C on his paper. 11:00 PM! As if I don't have a life outside of school. I was right in the middle of a very intense game of Scrabble with my husband, too. I had the letters for 'quixotic' but nowhere to play it. Such a shame. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't get unusually energized for days at a time. I'm just my regular self, trying to keep up with everything.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still as a child?\n\nPatient: As a child? Oh, absolutely. Even as a little girl, my report cards always said the exact same thing—that I was a very bright student, but I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. My mother used to joke that I wore a permanent path in the floorboards between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. \n\nSpeaking of pencil sharpeners, you know, the old metal ones that were screwed into the wall right by the chalkboard? The ones you really had to crank? Goodness, I miss the smell of the cedar shavings from those. Kids today don't even use wooden pencils anymore. It's all mechanical, or they're just typing away on their Chromebooks. I swear, the penmanship of my current sophomores is an absolute tragedy. Half the time I can't even read their assignments if they do happen to write them out by hand. They don't even teach cursive in most of the elementary schools in our district now, which just breaks my heart. I mean, how are they going to sign their names on a mortgage or a marriage license someday? Just print it in block letters? My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, would be rolling in her grave. She was an absolute stickler for the Palmer Method. We had to do those loops over and over on that special lined paper... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Oh! Right, yes. I was always up and about as a kid, constantly needing to sharpen my pencil or just moving around the classroom."
  }
]

Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, I don't know if it's an uncomfortable feeling exactly, but my legs are definitely always busy. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays—and oh, don't even get me started on the essays this year. The grammar is just atrocious. I don't know what they teach them in middle school anymore, but it certainly isn't punctuation! I had one student, bless his heart, who wrote an entire page about the French Revolution as a single run-on sentence. \n\nBut anyway, when I'm sitting there trying to get through the grading, or even when I'm in church on Sundays, I'll catch myself tapping my foot constantly. Or I'll be clicking my red pen, just click-click-click, and shifting around in my seat so much that I actually have to stop and remind myself to just settle down. The pews at our church are terribly hard, though. They haven't replaced the cushions since the late nineties, I swear. My husband always insists on sitting on the aisle because he likes the legroom, but I just end up squirming through the whole sermon.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nEnergized? Euphoric? Oh, heavens no. Writing that thesis nearly did me in. I wrote it on the symbolism in 19th-century American literature, specifically Nathaniel Hawthorne. You know, everyone focuses on *The Scarlet Letter*, but his short stories are really where he shines. *Young Goodman Brown*? Brilliant. I try to get my sophomores to appreciate it, but getting them to read anything that isn't on a screen these days is like pulling teeth. \n\nI actually confiscated three cell phones just yesterday during second period. Three! And then the parents complain when the kids miss the material. One mother emailed me at 11:00 PM on a Tuesday demanding to know why her son got a C on his paper. 11:00 PM! As if I don't have a life outside of school. I was right in the middle of a very intense game of Scrabble with my husband, too. I had the letters for 'quixotic' but nowhere to play it. I'm just my regular self, trying to keep up with everything.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nAs a child? Oh, absolutely. Even as a little girl, my report cards always said the exact same thing—that I was a very bright student, but I just could not stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. My mother used to joke that I wore a permanent path in the floorboards between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. \n\nSpeaking of pencil sharpeners, you know, the old metal ones that were screwed into the wall right by the chalkboard? The ones you really had to crank? Goodness, I miss the smell of the cedar shavings from those. Kids today don't even use wooden pencils anymore. It's all mechanical, or they're just typing away on their Chromebooks. I swear, the penmanship of my current sophomores is an absolute tragedy. Half the time I can't even read their assignments if they do happen to write them out by hand. They don't even teach cursive in most of the elementary schools in our district now, which just breaks my heart. I mean, how are they going to sign their names on a mortgage or a marriage license someday? Just print it in block letters? My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, would be rolling in her grave. She was an absolute stickler for the Palmer Method. We had to do those loops over and over on that special lined paper... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Oh! Right, yes.
